<figcaption><p>Aldolase A undergoes de novo translation and mobilizes from the cytoskeleton in response to Reelin stimulation. <strong><em>A</em></strong>, Representative immunoblots of DIV 7 neuronal cortical lysates treated with the mock control, 50 nM Reelin, 100 nM GST-RAP for 2 h prior to Reelin, or 50 µM LY294002 for 30 min prior to Reelin stimulation for aldolase A and β-actin. <strong><em>B</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification of aldolase A normalized to mock control showed an increase in aldolase A protein levels after Reelin stimulation (one-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(3, 29)</sub> = 8.998; <em>p</em> = 0.0002). <em>n</em> = 7–9 individual replicates. <strong><em>C</em></strong>, Representative immunoblots of DIV 7 neuronal lysates treated with mock control (Lanes 1–3), 50 nM Reelin (Lanes 4–6), or 40 µM anisomycin for 45 min prior to Reelin stimulation (Lanes 7–9) for aldolase A and β-actin. <strong><em>D</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification of aldolase A normalized to mock control showed an increase in aldolase A protein levels after Reelin stimulation but not in anisomycin-treated neurons (one-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(2, 25)</sub> = 4.275; <em>p</em> = 0.0253). <em>n</em> = 8–10 individual replicates. <strong><em>E</em></strong>, Representative immunoblots of DIV 7 neuronal lysates collected from neurons that were treated with 50 µM MG132 for 4 h prior to mock (Lanes 1–3) or Reelin stimulation (Lanes 4–6) and subjected to immunoblotting for aldolase A and β-actin. <em>n</em> = 3 individual replicates. <strong><em>F</em></strong>, Representative immunoblots of DIV 7 neuronal lysates treated with the mock control, 50 nM Reelin, 100 nM GST-RAP for 2 h prior to Reelin, or 50 µM LY294002 for 30 min prior to Reelin stimulation and subjected to digitonin permeabilization (Lanes 1–8). Supernatant (SN) and cell lysate were subjected to immunoblotting for aldolase A and β-actin as indicated. <strong><em>G</em></strong>, Quantification of aldolase A in the diffusible fraction (supernatant) to immobile (cell lysate) fraction show an increase in aldolase A in response to Reelin stimulation (one-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(3, 41)</sub> = 10.45; <em>p</em> &lt; 0.0001). <em>n</em> = 9–15 individual replicates using one-way ANOVA, Tukey's multiple comparisons.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Previous studies had shown that aldolase A is mobilized from the actin cytoskeleton through PI3K activation in epithelial cells (<a href="#B38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38">Hu et al., 2016</a>). To determine whether aldolase A is mobilized from the actin cytoskeleton through Reelin-PI3K signaling in primary murine cortical neurons, we performed a digitonin permeabilization assay to allow for efflux of diffusible aldolase A and estimate the fraction of aldolase A in the soluble versus immobilized state based on separate collection of supernatant and cell lysates, respectively. DIV 7 neurons were treated with either mock, Reelin, RAP, or LY294002 prior to Reelin stimulation and subsequently permeabilized with digitonin for 5 min at 4°C. We found a 35% increase in aldolase A in the supernatant fraction induced by Reelin stimulation indicating an increase in mobilized aldolase A when compared with the mock control (<em>p </em>= 0.0337; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5<em>F</em>,<em>G</em></a>, Lanes 3–4). Both RAP and LY294002 prevented the Reelin-mediated effect on aldolase A mobilization. In fact, LY294002 reduced aldolase A mobilization by 39% compared with the mock control (<em>p </em>= 0.0111; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5<em>F</em>,<em>G</em></a>, Lanes 7–8). We did not detect any aldolase A in the supernatant from neurons that were not permeabilized with digitonin which served as a negative control for the assay (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5<em>F</em></a>, Lanes 9–12). In summary, these results indicate that the effects of Reelin on aldolase A levels and mobilization from the cytoskeleton are dependent on the activation of PI3K.</p></section><section id="s3F"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Aldolase A functions downstream of Reelin signaling in regulating dendrite growth</h3>
<p>To directly test whether aldolase A functions downstream of Reelin signaling, we infected primary murine hippocampal neurons with lentiviral <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA targeting sequences in the 3′-UTR region at DIV 1. We found that <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA efficiently downregulated aldolase A protein expression at DIV 7 and resulted in a significant 64% knockdown compared with <em>scrambled</em> (<em>scr</em>) control shRNA (<em>p </em>= 0.0179; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6<em>A</em>,<em>B</em></a>, Lane 2). In contrast, infection of lentiviral <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA in neurons had no effect on β-actin levels (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6<em>A</em>,<em>B</em></a>). Since aldolase A is one of the two aldolase isozymes that is expressed in the brain, we also immunoblotted for aldolase C and found no difference in aldolase C levels demonstrating that <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA is specific to aldolase A (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6<em>A</em>,<em>B</em></a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="JN-RM-0072-24F6"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11484552_jneuro-44-e0072242024-g006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5501/11484552/69640dccb9e2/jneuro-44-e0072242024-g006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="900" width="747" alt="Figure 6."></a></p>

<figcaption><p>Aldolase A is necessary for Reelin-mediated neurite outgrowth and arborization. Primary murine hippocampal neurons on DIV 1 were infected with <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA and analyzed at DIV 7. <strong><em>A</em></strong>, Representative immunoblots showing aldolase A and aldolase C levels from DIV 7 neuronal lysate after infection with <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA. β-actin served as loading control. <strong><em>B</em></strong>, Bar graph quantifications of aldolase A, aldolase C, and β-actin expression normalized to <em>scr</em> shRNA showed a decreased protein level specifically for aldolase A (unpaired <em>t</em> test; <em>t</em> = 3.874; df = 4; <em>p </em>= 0.0179), but not for aldolase C (unpaired <em>t</em> test; <em>t</em> = 0.3822; df = 6; <em>p </em>= 0.7155) or β-actin (unpaired <em>t</em> test; <em>t</em> = 1.068; df = 6; <em>p </em>= 0.3267). <em>n</em> = 3 individual replicates. <strong><em>C</em></strong>, Representative images of DIV 7 neurons infected with <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA and stimulated with either the mock control or 50 nM Reelin once a day for 72 h starting at DIV 4 and immunostained against MAP2 at DIV 7. Scale bar, 12.5 µm. <strong><em>D</em></strong>, Sholl analysis comparing neuronal arborization complexity between <em>scr</em> and <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA with mock and Reelin treatment showed reduced dendritic growth, and arborization in aldolase A knockdown neurons and Reelin stimulation had no effect in neurons following aldolase A knockdown (two-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(54, 2,793)</sub> = 21.80; <em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001). <em>n</em> = 3 independent experiments with <em>n</em> = 30–42 cells per condition analyzed. <strong><em>E</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification of the average total neurite sum length (one-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(3, 157)</sub> = 89.41; <em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001). <strong><em>F</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification of the average maximum neurite sum length (one-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(3, 158)</sub> = 46.06; <em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001). <strong><em>G</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification of the average minimum neurite length (one-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(3, 158)</sub> = 5.149; <em>p </em>= 0.0020). For <strong><em>E–G</em></strong>, <em>n</em> = 3 independent experiments with <em>n</em> = 32–44 cells per condition analyzed using one-way ANOVA, Tukey's multiple comparisons. ns, not significant. See Extended Data <a href="#fig6-1" class="usa-link">Figure 6-1</a> for more details.</p></figcaption></figure><section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="fig6-1"><div class="caption p">
<span>Figure 6-1</span><p>Aldolase A is necessary for Reelin-mediated neurite outgrowth and arborization. Primary murine neurons on DIV 1 were infected with <em>scr</em> or with two pooled <em>Aldoa</em> shRNAs and analyzed at DIV 7. <strong><em>A,</em></strong> Representative images of DIV 7 neurons infected with <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA and stimulated with either mock control or 50 nM Reelin once a day for 72  h starting at DIV 4 and immunostained against MAP2 at DIV 7. Scale bar = 20 μm. <strong><em>B,</em></strong> Sholl analysis comparing neuronal arborization complexity between <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA with mock and Reelin treatment showed reduced dendritic growth and arborization in aldolase A knockdown neurons and Reelin stimulation had no effect in neurons following aldolase A knockdown [two-way ANOVA, F (42, 1740) = 11.86, p &lt; 0.0001]. <strong><em>C,</em></strong> Bar graph quantification of the average total neurite sum length [one-way ANOVA, F (3, 116) = 11.28, p &lt; 0.0001]. <strong><em>D,</em></strong> Bar graph quantification of the average maximum neurite sum length [one-way ANOVA, F (3, 116) = 12.30, p &lt; 0.0001]. <strong><em>E,</em></strong> Bar graph quantification of the average minimum neurite length [one-way ANOVA, F (3, 116) = 6.502, p = 0.0004]. n = 2 independent experiments with n = 30 cells per condition analyzed using Tukey’s Multiple Comparisons. ns = not significant. Download <a href="/articles/instance/11484552/bin/jneuro-44-e0072242024-s003.tif" class="usa-link" id="f6-1">Figure 6-1, TIF file</a><sup> (1.1MB, tif) </sup>.</p>
</div></section><p>To investigate whether aldolase A functions downstream of Reelin signaling on dendritic branching, primary murine hippocampal neurons were infected at DIV 1 with <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA, and starting at DIV 4, neurons were treated with 50 nM Reelin or the mock control once a day for 72 h and immunolabeled against dendritic marker, MAP2 at DIV 7. We performed Sholl analysis to examine global changes in neuronal morphology, specifically the complexity of the dendritic arbor and the overall pattern of arborization. Sholl analysis revealed that shRNA knockdown of aldolase A greatly reduced dendritic growth where it shifted the distribution downward and to the left compared with <em>scr</em> shRNA neurons, indicating both a decrease in the number and length of the neurites (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6<em>C</em>,<em>D</em></a>). The average total neurite length was 49.5% shorter in neurons infected with <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA compared with <em>scr</em> shRNA-infected neurons (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6<em>E</em></a>). Similarly, the average maximum neurite length was decreased by 40% in <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA-infected neurons (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6<em>F</em></a>) but had no effect on the average minimum neurite length between <em>scr</em> and <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA-infected neurons (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6<em>G</em></a>). Meanwhile, Reelin induced a 32% increase in the average total neurite length in <em>scr</em> shRNA neurons compared with the mock control (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6<em>E</em></a>). Similarly, the average maximum length of the longest neurite was increased by 31% following Reelin stimulation (<em>p </em>= 0.0002; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6<em>F</em></a>) but had no effect in the average minimum neurite length (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6<em>G</em></a>). Interestingly, Reelin stimulation had no effect in neurons following aldolase A knockdown compared with <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA-infected neurons alone (<em>p </em>= 0.3870 neurite sum length, <em>p </em>= 0.8237 maximum neurite length, and <em>p </em>= 0.9864 minimum neurite length; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6<em>E</em>–<em>G</em></a>). To exclude any potential off-target effects, we also examined at least two other <em>Aldoa</em> shRNAs (targeting different sequences within the ORF of <em>Aldoa</em>) and found a similar phenotype (Extended Data <a href="#fig6-1" class="usa-link">Fig. 6-1</a>) further confirming that aldolase A is downstream of Reelin signaling and is essential for Reelin-induced dendritic outgrowth.</p></section><section id="s3G"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Aldolase A knockdown disrupts neuronal morphology in vivo</h3>
<p>Since we found aldolase A functions downstream of the Reelin signaling pathway, we next asked whether aldolase A knockdown impacts neuronal migration and architecture in vivo. To test this, we performed IUE with GFP-tagged <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA on mouse embryos at E15.5 and analyzed both the position of GFP-positive cells within the cortical layers and morphology located in the primary somatosensory cortex at P14 to account for uniform distribution of cells along the rostrocaudal axis. As expected, we found most GFP-positive neurons electroporated with <em>scr</em> shRNA were in upper Layers 2 and 3 that expressed Satb2 which we used as a marker to distinguish cortical layers for analysis (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7<em>A</em>,<em>B</em></a>). Interestingly, we found neurons electroporated with <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA distributed deeper within Layers 2 and 3. We, therefore, measured the distance of the soma from the pia and compared neurons electroporated with <em>scr</em> and <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA. We found neurons electroporated with <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA were 57.75 µm deeper in the cortex compared with <em>scr</em> electroporated neurons (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7<em>C</em></a>). We investigated further by examining the lamina distribution of lower- and upper-level cortical neurons by immunostaining for Brn2, a marker for Layers 2/3 and 5. We found that <em>Aldoa</em> knockdown neurons colocalized with Brn2 similar to <em>scr</em> shRNA electroporated neurons suggesting that aldolase A knockdown did not affect the neuronal lamina distribution of upper-level cortical neurons (Extended Data <a href="#fig7-1" class="usa-link">Fig. 7-1</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="JN-RM-0072-24F7"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11484552_jneuro-44-e0072242024-g007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5501/11484552/606feefc402c/jneuro-44-e0072242024-g007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="679" width="797" alt="Figure 7."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/JN-RM-0072-24F7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Aldolase A is required for neuronal arborization and apical dendrite polarity in the mouse cortex. Mouse embryos were electroporated in utero with GFP-tagged <em>scr</em> control or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA at E15.5 and analyzed at P14. <strong><em>A</em></strong>, Representative images of brain sections electroporated with GFP-tagged <em>scr</em> control or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA and immunostained against GFP and Satb2 to visualize electroporated cells and marker to distinguish cortical layers in the somatosensory S1 cortex, respectively. Scale bar, 175 µm. <strong><em>B</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification showing the percentage of GFP-positive neurons across cortical layers between <em>scr</em> and <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA electroporated neurons (two-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(2, 12)</sub> = 7.070; <em>p </em>= 0.0094). <em>n</em> = 3 mice per group with <em>n</em> = 402–639 cells per group analyzed. <strong><em>C</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification showing the average soma depth of GFP-positive electroporated neurons from the pia showed aldolase A knockdown neurons reside deeper in the cortex compared with <em>scr</em> electroporated neurons (unpaired <em>t</em> test; <em>t</em> = 7.889; df = 556; <em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001). <em>n</em> = 3 brains per group with <em>n</em> = 264–294 cells per group analyzed. <strong><em>D</em></strong>, Representative images of GFP-positive neurons in Layers 2/3 of S1 from brains electroporated at E15.5 with <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA showing the disrupted morphology and arborization of aldolase A knockdown neurons compared with <em>scr</em> control. Scale bar, 25 µm. <strong><em>E</em></strong>, Sholl analysis showed aldolase A knockdown neurons have shorter total neurite length and less neuronal arborization compared with <em>scr</em> control neurons. A two-way ANOVA was used to determine the statistical significance of the interaction between the length of the neurites and number of crossings at each radius for each treatment condition (two-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(222, 14,718)</sub> = 14.06; <em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001). <em>n</em> = 3 brains per condition and <em>n</em> = 31–37 neurons per condition analyzed. <strong><em>F</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification showing aldolase A knockdown neurons led to a decrease in the average neurite sum length compared with <em>scr</em> control electroporated neurons (unpaired <em>t</em> test; <em>t</em> = 6.478; df = 63; <em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001). <strong><em>G</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification of the average maximum neurite length between <em>scr</em> and <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA electroporated neurons (unpaired <em>t</em> test; <em>t</em> = 7.272; df = 62; <em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001). <strong><em>H</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification of the average minimum neurite length between <em>scr</em> and <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA electroporated neurons (unpaired <em>t</em> test; <em>t</em> = 2.559; df = 55; <em>p </em>= 0.0133). For <strong><em>F–H</em></strong>, <em>n</em> = 3 brains per group with <em>n</em> = 25–35 neurons analyzed. <strong><em>I</em></strong>, Representative images of GFP-positive neurons in Layers II/III of S1 from brains electroporated with either <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA (left panel) and IMARIS generated 3D wire structure reconstruction of the same neurons (right panel) showing their morphology and apical neurite orientation and length is altered in aldolase A knockdown neurons. Scale bar, 25 µm. <strong><em>J</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification showing a reduction in the average length of apical neurites for aldolase A knockdown neurons (unpaired <em>t</em> test; <em>t</em> = 6.164; df = 65; <em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001). <em>n</em> = 3 brains per group and <em>n</em> = 33–34 neurons analyzed. <strong><em>K</em></strong>, The percentage of GFP-positive neurons with an apical dendrite oriented between 0–30, 31–60, and 61–90° to the pia were quantified. Quantification revealed control electroporated neurons have apical dendrites oriented mostly within 0–30° compared with aldolase A knockdown neurons (two-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(2, 12)</sub> = 9.129; <em>p </em>= 0.0039). <em>n</em> = 3 brains per group with <em>n</em> = 30–35 neurons analyzed using two-way ANOVA, Šídák's multiple comparisons. See Extended Data <a href="#fig7-1" class="usa-link">Figure 7-1</a> for more details.</p></figcaption></figure><section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="fig7-1"><div class="caption p">
<span>Figure 7-1</span><p>Mouse embryos were electroporated <em>in utero</em> with GFP-tagged <em>scr</em> control or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA at E15.5 and analyzed at P14. Representative images of brain sections electroporated with GFP-tagged <em>scr</em> control or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA and immunostained against GFP and Brn2 to visualize electroporated cells and marker to distinguish upper cortical layers II/III in somatosensory S1 cortex. White dots indicate some of the GFP electroporated neurons colocalized for GFP and Brn2. Scale bar = 200 μm. Download <a href="/articles/instance/11484552/bin/jneuro-44-e0072242024-s004.tif" class="usa-link" id="f7-1">Figure 7-1, TIF file</a><sup> (7.2MB, tif) </sup>.</p>
</div></section><p>We next examined whether aldolase A is necessary for proper neuronal growth and arborization and performed Sholl analysis on neurons electroporated with <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA. Sholl analysis revealed aldolase A knockdown neurons exhibited a significant reduction in both dendritic length and arbor complexity compared with <em>scr</em> electroporated neurons (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7<em>D</em>,<em>E</em></a>). The average total neurite length was 65.6% shorter in aldolase A knockdown neurons compared with <em>scr</em> electroporated neurons (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7<em>F</em></a>). Similarly, the average maximum neurite length was decreased by 53.4% in <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA electroporated neurons (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7<em>G</em></a>). Interestingly, we found a small and significant increase in the average minimum neurite length between <em>scr</em> and <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA electroporated neurons (<em>p </em>= 0.0133; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7<em>H</em></a>).</p>
<p>Not only did aldolase A knockdown neurons have a significant decrease in neurite length and arborization, it was apparent they also lacked the apical–basal polarity typical of cortical pyramidal neurons (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7<em>I</em></a>). As such, we measured the length and orientation of the apical dendrite in GFP-positive neurons of <em>scr</em> control and <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA electroporated neurons at P14. Our results demonstrate that aldolase A knockdown caused a 69.24% decrease in the length of the apical dendrite when compared with controls (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7<em>J</em></a>). We examined apical dendrite orientation and calculated this as an angle in relation to the pial surface and binned the data into three groups: 0–30, 31–60, and 61–90° from the pial surface. Nearly 94.83% of GFP-positive neurons electroporated with <em>scr</em> control had their apical dendrite oriented between 0 and 30° to the pia. However, only 57.26% of aldolase A knockdown neurons kept this normal, apical orientation (<em>p </em>= 0.017; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7<em>K</em></a>). Instead, we found nearly 33.2% of aldolase A knockdown neurons had an apical dendrite oriented between 31 and 60° and 9.55% had their apical dendrite between 61 and 90°, both trending higher when compared with <em>scr</em> control neurons. Altogether, these data support the conclusion that aldolase A is necessary for proper neuron arborization and is particularly important for the growth and orientation of the apical dendrite of pyramidal neurons in the mouse cortex.</p></section><section id="s3H"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Aldolase A requires actin-binding activity for proper neuronal arborization</h3>
<p>Because aldolase A serves as both a glycolytic enzyme and actin-binding protein, it is in a unique position to regulate the metabolic demands and the structural rearrangements of the cytoskeleton necessary for the energy-intensive process of neurite growth and arborization. To determine which functional activity of aldolase A is required for proper neuronal arborization, we examined two aldolase mouse variants defective in either glycolytic (D33S) or actin-binding activity (R42A) that have been previously characterized in non-neuronal cells (<a href="#B73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73">Wang et al., 1996</a>; <a href="#B64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64">Ritterson Lew and Tolan, 2012</a>, <a href="#B65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65">2013</a>; <a href="#B38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38">Hu et al., 2016</a>). The D33S aldolase A variant is catalytically dead but retains F-actin–binding activity, whereas the R42A variant abolishes the F-actin–binding activity but retains glycolytic activity. We generated three lentiviral mouse aldolase A constructs, WT, D33S, and R42A, and we titered each lentivirus uniformly to perform rescue experiments of aldolase A's knockdown effects on dendritic arborization. Primary murine hippocampal neurons were infected on DIV 1, and we performed immunolabeling against MAP2 at DIV 7. We compared the effects of <em>scr</em> shRNA, <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA (targeting 3′-UTR), <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA rescued with mouse aldolase A WT, catalytically dead D33S, or actin-binding–deficient R42A variant. The <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA-mediated knockdown of aldolase A decreased neurite arborization compared with <em>scr</em> shRNA (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8<em>A</em>,<em>B</em></a>). Both aldolase A WT and catalytically dead D33S rescued the <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA phenotype, whereas the actin-binding–deficient R42A was unable to rescue the neurite arborization effects of aldolase A knockdown on dendritic outgrowth (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8<em>A</em>,<em>B</em></a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="JN-RM-0072-24F8"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11484552_jneuro-44-e0072242024-g008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5501/11484552/45fa5679fe04/jneuro-44-e0072242024-g008.jpg" loading="lazy" height="682" width="797" alt="Figure 8."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/JN-RM-0072-24F8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Aldolase A requires actin-binding function to regulate proper neuronal arborization. <strong><em>A</em></strong>, Representative images of DIV 7 neurons immunostained against MAP2 infected with <em>scr</em> shRNA, <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA, or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA rescue with aldolase A WT, D33S, or R42A variant on DIV1. Scale bar, 25 µm. <strong><em>B</em></strong>, Sholl analysis showed <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA-infected neurons exhibit reduced dendritic length and arborization compared with the <em>scr</em> control. Lentiviral infection with aldolase WT or D33S variant rescued <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA-induced phenotype, while R42A variant was unable to rescue the phenotype. Two-way ANOVA analysis used to determine statistical significance of the interaction between neurite length and number of intersections at each radius for each condition (two-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(72, 3,230)</sub> = 14.70; <em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001). <em>n</em> = 3 independent experiments with <em>n</em> = 34–36 cells analyzed per condition. <strong><em>C</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification of the average total neurite sum length one-way ANOVA used to determine statistical significance (one-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(4, 170)</sub> = 63.91; <em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001). <strong><em>D</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification of the average maximum neurite sum length (one-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(4, 168)</sub> = 36.06; <em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001). <strong><em>E</em></strong>, Bar graph quantification of the average minimum neurite length (one-way ANOVA; <em>F</em><sub>(4, 165)</sub> = 4.176; <em>p </em>= 0.003). For <strong><em>C–E</em></strong>, <em>n</em> = 3 independent experiments with <em>n</em> = 33–36 neurons analyzed per condition using one-way or two-way ANOVA, Tukey's multiple comparisons. ns, not significant.</p></figcaption></figure><p>We systematically measured the average total, maximum, and minimum neurite length between the conditions. The average total neurite length was 57.4% shorter in neurons infected with <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA compared with <em>scr</em>-infected neurons (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8<em>C</em></a>). Lentiviral infection with aldolase A WT and catalytically dead D33S variant fully rescued the effects of aldolase A knockdown phenotype (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8<em>C</em></a>). In contrast, the actin-binding–deficient R42A variant led to a 47.8% reduction in average total neurite length compared with <em>scr</em> shRNA and was unable to rescue the aldolase A knockdown phenotype (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8<em>C</em></a>). Similarly, the average maximum neurite length was decreased by 44.9% in <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA-infected neurons (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8<em>D</em></a>). Both aldolase A WT and D33S fully rescued the <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA phenotype (<em>p </em>&lt; 0.0001; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8<em>D</em></a>); however, the R42A variant was unable to rescue the maximum neurite length following aldolase A knockdown when compared with <em>scr</em> shRNA control (<em>p </em>= 0.0002; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8<em>D</em></a>). Likewise, the average minimum length of the shortest neurite was rescued by the catalytically dead D33S aldolase A variant (<em>p </em>= 0.0152; <a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8<em>E</em></a>). Together, these results provide strong evidence that the actin-binding function of aldolase A is a key regulator controlling dendritic outgrowth.</p></section></section><section id="s4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Discussion</h2>
<p>In this study, we showed Reelin selectively activates S6K1 phosphorylation through the PI3K/Akt and mTORC1 complex pathways during initial stages of axonal and dendritic outgrowth, a process that differs in mature cortical neurons in culture (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>). This aligns with previous studies that demonstrated Reelin signaling to PI3K/Akt is necessary for Reelin-induced neurite outgrowth (<a href="#B7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7">Beffert et al., 2002</a>; <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Jossin and Goffinet, 2007</a>; <a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Leemhuis et al., 2010</a>). Through an unbiased proteomics analysis to delineate between canonical and noncanonical Reelin signaling during dendritic outgrowth in primary murine cortical neurons, we found significant cross talk related to cytoskeleton regulation, neuron projection development, and actin filament-based processes (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>). Notably, gene sets influenced by the noncanonical Reelin pathway include protein translation, mRNA metabolic processes, and ribonucleoprotein complex biogenesis, highlighting distinct impacts on neuronal structure and function.</p>
<p>We explored how Reelin specifically influences actin and not α-tubulin dynamics in dendritic development at DIV 7 (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>). We showed that Reelin stimulation reduces F-actin intensity while increasing total β-actin intensity in developing neurites. The increase in β-actin was not corroborated by Western blot analysis, suggesting the changes we observed in immunocytochemistry might be due to an increase in monomeric actin following F-actin depolymerization and not due to de novo translation. Indeed, neurons treated with anisomycin, a translation inhibitor, had no effect on Reelin-induced β-actin intensity. Accompanying these changes was a reduction in cofilin serine3 phosphorylation (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>), indicating enhanced cofilin severing activity. This suggests that Reelin promotes cofilin-mediated actin depolymerization and rearrangement in neurites in vitro, in contrast to previous studies that indicated Reelin signaling through PI3K increased cofilin serine3 phosphorylation, stabilizing the actin cytoskeleton in the leading processes of migrating neurons during embryonic mouse brain development (<a href="#B17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17">Chai et al., 2009</a>; <a href="#B26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26">Frotscher et al., 2017</a>). We posit Reelin's dual role in modulating cofilin activity, either inhibiting or promoting cofilin severing activity, depending on the developmental stage of neurons. Reelin inhibits cofilin severing activity and stabilizes the actin cytoskeleton to act as a stop signal for migrating neurons which is required for orientation and directed migration of cortical neurons (<a href="#B17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17">Chai et al., 2009</a>), but it could also function to promote cofilin severing activity by decreasing cofilin serine3 phosphorylation to allow for actin rearrangements essential for neurite outgrowth and arborization in primary murine neuronal cultures.</p>
<p>Reelin is a commanding regulator of neuron growth during different stages of brain development, particularly in embryonic and postnatal stages. Previous studies have shown that dendritic growth of pyramidal cells is disrupted in both homozygous and heterozygous <em>reeler</em> mice (<a href="#B61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61">Pinto Lord and Caviness, 1979</a>; <a href="#B54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54">Niu et al., 2004</a>). Addition of Reelin in vitro has been shown to increase dendritic growth of hippocampal neurons in both WT and <em>reeler</em> mutant mice (<a href="#B54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54">Niu et al., 2004</a>; <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Jossin and Goffinet, 2007</a>; <a href="#B51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51">Matsuki et al., 2008</a>). This enhancement of dendritic growth by Reelin is evident during early brain development (<a href="#B53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53">Nichols and Olson, 2010</a>; <a href="#B46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46">Kupferman et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#B16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16">Chai et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#B43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43">Kohno et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#B56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56">O'Dell et al., 2015</a>). Our current study aligns with these findings, focusing on the in vitro effects of Reelin on the dendritic growth and branching of embryonic hippocampal neurons. However, Reelin exerts an opposing effect on dendritic growth in cortical pyramidal neurons and forebrain interneurons during postnatal development (<a href="#B78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78">Yabut et al., 2007</a>; <a href="#B18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18">Chameau et al., 2009</a>; <a href="#B32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32">Hamad et al., 2021</a>). Specifically, the N-terminal fragment of Reelin has been shown to control and restrict the postnatal maturation of apical dendrites in pyramidal cortical neurons, a process mediated by integrin receptors (<a href="#B18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18">Chameau et al., 2009</a>). This suggests that the postnatal effects of Reelin might be attributed to the differential impact of its proteolytic fragments interacting with noncanonical receptors. Our work contributes to the growing body of evidence on the role of Reelin in dendritic development. We provide insights into the proteomic changes through which both canonical and noncanonical Reelin pathways regulate dendrite growth in embryonic cultured neurons at DIV 7. However, it remains unclear whether these in vitro observations fully reflect the in vivo mechanisms and effects of Reelin on dendritic development across various stages of brain maturation. We also recognize that the actin cytoskeletal analysis of this study focuses on the canonical Reelin pathway. Therefore, future studies should examine the contribution of the noncanonical pathway to better understand the effects of actin dynamics in Reelin-induced dendrite growth.</p>
<p>Our proteomics screen identified aldolase A, a glycolytic enzyme and actin-binding protein, as a critical component in Reelin-mediated actin dynamics (<a href="#B4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4">Arnold and Pette, 1968</a>; <a href="#B3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3">Arnold et al., 1971</a>; <a href="#B73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73">Wang et al., 1996</a>; <a href="#B47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47">Kusakabe et al., 1997</a>; <a href="#B40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40">Jewett and Sibley, 2003</a>). We demonstrated that Reelin stimulates de novo translation of aldolase A, mobilizing soluble aldolase A from the cytoskeleton, linking aldolase A directly to Reelin signaling highlighting a potential role of aldolase A in promoting and stabilizing changes in actin dynamics regulating dendrite development (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>). We showed that downregulation of aldolase A in primary murine neurons led to shorter, less branched dendrites, and Reelin treatment was ineffective following aldolase A knockdown, indicating aldolase A is necessary for Reelin's effects on dendritic growth. Similar results were observed in cortical neurons in the developing mouse brain, with aberrant length, arborization, and orientation of the apical dendrite (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>), reinforcing the essential role of aldolase A in proper neuron development.</p>
<p>We further investigated aldolase A's dual functions in neurite outgrowth and demonstrated that the actin-binding ability of aldolase A, rather than its glycolytic function, is critical for neurite development using site-directed variants of aldolase A (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>). As previously characterized, the D33S aldolase variant is catalytically dead and retains F-actin–binding ability, while the R42A mutant is catalytically active and unable to bind F-actin (<a href="#B73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73">Wang et al., 1996</a>; <a href="#B65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65">Ritterson Lew and Tolan, 2013</a>; <a href="#B38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38">Hu et al., 2016</a>). We demonstrated that both the mouse WT and catalytically inactive D33S aldolase variant, which retains F-actin–binding ability, rescued the effects of <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA knockdown phenotype in primary murine neurons, while the R42A mutant, unable to bind F-actin, showed impaired neurite growth and arborization. This finding reinforces aldolase A's role in cell growth and motility, independent of its glycolytic activity, in non-neuronal cells as observed in previous studies (<a href="#B71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71">Tochio et al., 2010</a>; <a href="#B64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64">Ritterson Lew and Tolan, 2012</a>, <a href="#B65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65">2013</a>; <a href="#B29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29">Gizak et al., 2019</a>). Our data establish a direct link between aldolase A and neuronal development downstream of Reelin signaling, revealing a novel functional role for aldolase A in neurons involving actin-binding activity. This expands our understanding of how Reelin might regulate actin filament dynamics, particularly in relation to dendrite growth. Future studies to directly test aldolase A on F-actin dynamics affecting actin remodeling will be important to address.</p>
<p>In summary, our study elucidated how Reelin's canonical and noncanonical signaling pathways influence cytoskeletal development and organization relating to the actin filament-based process during dendrite outgrowth in vitro. Reelin stimulated actin remodeling dynamics by modifying changes in F-actin and β-actin intensity and decreasing n-cofilin phosphorylation. A decrease in n-cofilin phosphorylation likely promotes n-cofilin to sever F-actin, facilitating actin rearrangement. Since previous studies have shown that aldolase A and n-cofilin compete for the same binding site on F-actin (<a href="#B29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29">Gizak et al., 2019</a>), we propose a model where Reelin mobilizes aldolase A from the actin cytoskeleton, enabling cofilin-dependent F-actin depolymerization and rearrangement essential for neurite extension and arborization (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F9" class="usa-link">Fig. 9<em>A</em></a>). However, the implications of increased aldolase A expression and mobilization in the context of dendritic growth are not clear. It is plausible that the increased amount of aldolase A functions to stabilize F-actin and/or promotes increased actin branching through interactions with the N-WASP-Arp2/3 complex which is involved in the nucleation of new actin filaments (<a href="#B13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13">Buscaglia et al., 2006</a>; <a href="#B68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68">St-Jean et al., 2007</a>). Previous studies have linked aldolase A to Arp2/3-mediated actin polymerization through interactions with WASP in non-neuronal cells (<a href="#B21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21">Chiquete-Felix et al., 2009</a>; <a href="#B65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65">Ritterson Lew and Tolan, 2013</a>). Given that N-WASP-Arp2/3 complex is also involved in dendrite and dendritic spine formation (<a href="#B76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B76">Wegner et al., 2008</a>; <a href="#B67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67">San Miguel-Ruiz and Letourneau, 2014</a>; <a href="#B33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33">Hasegawa et al., 2022</a>), it is possible that dissociated and newly translated aldolase A binds activated WASP and guides it to actin filaments, acting as a node for Arp2/3 to start a new F-actin branch (<a href="#JN-RM-0072-24F9" class="usa-link">Fig. 9<em>B</em></a>). Future studies will investigate how Reelin fine-tunes the actin cytoskeleton, specifically through molecular players like aldolase A, to cooperate and work with other actin-binding proteins in controlling the structure and stability of F-actin networks. Understanding this mechanism will be crucial for understanding how dendrite morphology is shaped.</p>

<figcaption><p>Proposed model in how Reelin affect actin filament-based process during dendrite outgrowth in primary murine neurons. <strong><em>A</em></strong>, Reelin mobilizes aldolase A from actin and dephosphorylates cofilin which promotes cofilin to sever F-actin leading to a decrease in F-actin intensity. <strong><em>B</em></strong>, Testable hypotheses and implications of what increased expression and mobilization of aldolase A might mean in the context of dendritic growth. Increased amount of soluble aldolase A may function to stabilize F-actin and/or promote increased actin branching and elongation through interaction with the N-WASP-Arp2/3 complex, acting as a node to start a new actin branch. The model was created with <a href="http://www.BioRender.com" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">BioRender.com</a>, Agreement Number BK272QV43D.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="ref-list1" class="ref-list"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">References</h2>

<p class="font-secondary"><em>This section collects any data citations, data availability statements, or supplementary materials included in this article.</em></p>
<section id="_adsm93_" lang="en" class="supplementary-materials"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Materials</h3>
<section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="db_ds_supplementary-material1_reqid_"><div class="caption p">
<span>Figure 2-1</span><p>Label free quantification proteomics analysis of Reelin time-course treatment on wild type murine cortical neurons at DIV 7. Each column represents an individual sample with duplicate treatments grouped together. Heatmap showing differentially expressed features identified with an FDR &lt; 0.05. Feature intensity is represented as a Z-score between +2 (Yellow) and -2 (Blue), higher Z-scores correspond to greater relative expression while lower Z-scores correspond to lower relative expression. Dendrogram clustering groups treatment conditions by the similarity of the proteomic profile. Download <a href="/articles/instance/11484552/bin/jneuro-44-e0072242024-s001.tif" class="usa-link">Figure 2-1, TIF file</a><sup> (1.4MB, tif) </sup>.</p>
</div></section><section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="db_ds_supplementary-material2_reqid_"><div class="caption p">
<span>Figure 2-2</span><p>GST-RAP inhibits Dab1 phosphorylation. <strong><em>A,</em></strong> Coomassie showing fractions taken from GST-RAP production and purification. <strong><em>B,</em></strong> Representative immunoblot of tyrosine-phosphorylated Dab1 immunoprecipitated from DIV 7 primary murine cortical neurons demonstrating GST-RAP prevented Reelin-induced Dab1 phosphorylation. Neurons were treated with 50 nM Reelin or mock control for 30  min or 100 nM GST-RAP for 2  h prior to 30  min Reelin treatment. Download <a href="/articles/instance/11484552/bin/jneuro-44-e0072242024-s002.tif" class="usa-link">Figure 2-2, TIF file</a><sup> (318.7KB, tif) </sup>.</p>
</div></section><section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="db_ds_supplementary-material3_reqid_"><div class="caption p">
<span>Figure 6-1</span><p>Aldolase A is necessary for Reelin-mediated neurite outgrowth and arborization. Primary murine neurons on DIV 1 were infected with <em>scr</em> or with two pooled <em>Aldoa</em> shRNAs and analyzed at DIV 7. <strong><em>A,</em></strong> Representative images of DIV 7 neurons infected with <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA and stimulated with either mock control or 50 nM Reelin once a day for 72  h starting at DIV 4 and immunostained against MAP2 at DIV 7. Scale bar = 20 μm. <strong><em>B,</em></strong> Sholl analysis comparing neuronal arborization complexity between <em>scr</em> or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA with mock and Reelin treatment showed reduced dendritic growth and arborization in aldolase A knockdown neurons and Reelin stimulation had no effect in neurons following aldolase A knockdown [two-way ANOVA, F (42, 1740) = 11.86, p &lt; 0.0001]. <strong><em>C,</em></strong> Bar graph quantification of the average total neurite sum length [one-way ANOVA, F (3, 116) = 11.28, p &lt; 0.0001]. <strong><em>D,</em></strong> Bar graph quantification of the average maximum neurite sum length [one-way ANOVA, F (3, 116) = 12.30, p &lt; 0.0001]. <strong><em>E,</em></strong> Bar graph quantification of the average minimum neurite length [one-way ANOVA, F (3, 116) = 6.502, p = 0.0004]. n = 2 independent experiments with n = 30 cells per condition analyzed using Tukey’s Multiple Comparisons. ns = not significant. Download <a href="/articles/instance/11484552/bin/jneuro-44-e0072242024-s003.tif" class="usa-link">Figure 6-1, TIF file</a><sup> (1.1MB, tif) </sup>.</p>
</div></section><section class="sm xbox font-sm" id="db_ds_supplementary-material4_reqid_"><div class="caption p">
<span>Figure 7-1</span><p>Mouse embryos were electroporated <em>in utero</em> with GFP-tagged <em>scr</em> control or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA at E15.5 and analyzed at P14. Representative images of brain sections electroporated with GFP-tagged <em>scr</em> control or <em>Aldoa</em> shRNA and immunostained against GFP and Brn2 to visualize electroporated cells and marker to distinguish upper cortical layers II/III in somatosensory S1 cortex. White dots indicate some of the GFP electroporated neurons colocalized for GFP and Brn2. Scale bar = 200 μm. Download <a href="/articles/instance/11484552/bin/jneuro-44-e0072242024-s004.tif" class="usa-link">Figure 7-1, TIF file</a><sup> (7.2MB, tif) </sup>.</p>
</div></section></section></section></section><footer class="p courtesy-note font-secondary font-sm text-center"><hr class="headless">
